She arrived in time to help with funeral arrangements and was present when the family viewed the body and recounted the circumstances of the attack in graphic detail, with local news replaying footage of the crime scene. Returning to New York, she tried to regain her structured routine of Agile projects, salsa nights, and hikes along the Hudson, but she found that anything related to buses, crowded public transit, or news about violence in Latin America became overwhelming, and she began to pull back from community events that might bring up conversations about the incident. After her manager noted a drop in her usual engagement at work and her husband pointed out how much she was avoiding trips back to Guatemala and even planning them, she brought up her ongoing distress with her primary care provider, who referred her to psychiatry when it was clear the trauma around her cousin’s death was still affecting her life and relationships.\n- Chief Complaint: “I go out of my way to avoid anything that reminds me of what happened to my cousin, even if it means cutting out parts of my life.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to live for salsa nights and weekend hikes, but now even when I force myself to go I just feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life and would rather just be home on the couch scrolling mindlessly.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie in bed exhausted but wired, dozing off for an hour or two and then jolting awake from these vivid dreams about buses or my family back home, and after that I just toss and turn until my alarm goes off and I feel like I never really slept.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—at my desk, on the subway, even cooking dinner—I’ll suddenly see my cousin’s body in the coffin or hear my aunt describing the shooting, like a horrible clip playing in my head that I can’t pause, and my chest tightens and I just freeze for a minute.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I avoid anything that might bring it up—no buses, no Spanish news, I’ve stopped going to Guatemalan community events and even muted family group chats—because I’m terrified that if I hear one more story or see one more image I’ll completely fall apart.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I remember the airport, the wake, and the burial in sharp detail, but there are big blank spots around when we viewed his body and some of the conversations about what happened on the bus, like whole chunks of that day are just missing when I try to piece it together.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Underneath everything I do, there’s this constant mix of dread and guilt, like I failed him by not being there or not getting him out of Guatemala sooner, and it colors even ordinary days so they all feel heavy and slightly wrong.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when something objectively good happens—like my team shipping a big release or my daughter getting an award at school—I smile and say the right things, but inside it feels muted and distant, like I’m going through the motions and can’t actually feel happy or close to anyone.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): Since all this happened I’ve caught myself driving way too fast on the highway, drinking more than I should on weeknights, and taking on impossible workloads at the office, almost like I don’t care what happens to me as long as I don’t have to sit still with my thoughts.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone walks up behind me at work or a car backfires on the street, I jump out of my skin—my heart races, my whole body tenses, and even after I realize it’s nothing, it takes a long time for me to calm back down.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My performance reviews at work have slipped because I miss deadlines and avoid collaborative meetings, and socially I’ve pulled back so much from my Guatemalan I also jump out of my skin at sudden noises or if someone walks up behind me at work, and it takes a long time to calm down. This constant exhaustion has caused my work performance to slip with missed deadlines, and I've pulled back from my family because I just have no energy left.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned avoiding reminders and having intrusive thoughts, but can you tell me if these thoughts are always about your cousin's death or if they sometimes involve fears of harm coming to yourself or others in a way that doesn't directly relate to your cousin's passing?\n\nPatient: Yes, the intrusive thoughts and mental images are always directly related to my cousin's death, like suddenly seeing him in his coffin or hearing my aunt describe the shooting. I don't have unrelated fears of harm coming to myself or other people, though I do carry a constant, heavy sense of dread and guilt specifically tied to feeling like I failed him."

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thank you, doctor. Honestly, I'm here because I find myself going out of my way to avoid absolutely anything that reminds me of what happened to my cousin. It's gotten to the point where I'm cutting out important parts of my own life just to keep from remembering.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're reminded of what happened to your cousin, do you experience any distressing thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations?\n\nPatient: When I'm reminded, I suddenly see my cousin's body in the coffin or hear my aunt describing the shooting like a horrible clip playing in my head, and my chest tightens so much that I just freeze. I'm immediately flooded with this heavy dread and guilt, feeling like I somehow failed him by not getting him out of Guatemala sooner.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and images of your cousin's death, and have they been present every day since it happened?\n\nPatient: These intrusive images have been happening steadily since I came back from Guatemala after his funeral, which was a little over a year ago—about 14 months now. They happen almost every day, popping up out of nowhere while I'm at my desk working or just cooking dinner.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or repetitive behaviors in an attempt to reduce your distress or prevent something bad from happening, or are your actions mainly focused on avoiding reminders of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like that. I also jump out of my skin at sudden noises or if someone walks up behind me at work, and it takes a long time to calm down. This constant exhaustion has caused my work performance to slip with missed deadlines, and I've pulled back from my family because I just have no energy left.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned avoiding reminders and having intrusive thoughts, but can you tell me if these thoughts are always about your cousin's death or if they sometimes involve fears of harm coming to yourself or others in a way that doesn't directly relate to your cousin's passing?\n\nPatient: Yes, the intrusive thoughts and mental images are always directly related to my cousin's death, like suddenly seeing him in his coffin or hearing my aunt describe the shooting. I don't have unrelated fears of harm coming to myself or other people, though I do carry a constant, heavy sense of dread and guilt specifically tied to feeling like I failed him.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate on what specifically makes you feel like you failed your cousin, is it something you did or didn't do before or after his death that contributes to this overwhelming sense of guilt?\n\nPatient: I feel a deep sense of guilt because I feel like I failed him by not being there when it happened, or by not helping him get out of Guatemala sooner. This heavy feeling of dread sits underneath everything I do now, coloring even my ordinary days so they all feel heavy and slightly wrong. It's just this constant weight that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you had acted differently, your cousin's death could have been prevented, and is this belief what drives your feeling of having failed him?"

live for salsa nights and weekend hikes, but now even when I force myself to go I just feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life and would rather just be home on the couch scrolling mindlessly.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I lie in bed exhausted but wired, dozing off for an hour or two and then jolting awake from these vivid dreams about buses or my family back home, and after that I just toss and turn until my alarm goes off and I feel like I never really slept.\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): Out of nowhere—at my desk, on the subway, even cooking dinner—I’ll suddenly see my cousin’s body in the coffin or hear my aunt describing the shooting, like a horrible clip playing in my head that I can’t pause, and my chest tightens and I just freeze for a minute.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I avoid anything that might bring it up—no buses, no Spanish news, I’ve stopped going to Guatemalan community events and even muted family group chats—because I’m terrified that if I hear one more story or see one more image I’ll completely fall apart.\n- S071 (Trauma_Related_Amnesia): I remember the airport, the wake, and the burial in sharp detail, but there are big blank spots around when we viewed his body and some of the conversations about what happened on the bus, like whole chunks of that day are just missing when I try to piece it together.\n- S074 (Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State): Underneath everything I do, there’s this constant mix of dread and guilt, like I failed him by not being there or not getting him out of Guatemala sooner, and it colors even ordinary days so they all feel heavy and slightly wrong.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when something objectively good happens—like my team shipping a big release or my daughter getting an award at school—I smile and say the right things, but inside it feels muted and distant, like I’m going through the motions and can’t actually feel happy or close to anyone.\n- S078 (Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior): Since all this happened I’ve caught myself driving way too fast on the highway, drinking more than I should on weeknights, and taking on impossible workloads at the office, almost like I don’t care what happens to me as long as I don’t have to sit still with my thoughts.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): If someone walks up behind me at work or a car backfires on the street, I jump out of my skin—my heart races, my whole body tenses, and even after I realize it’s nothing, it takes a long time for me to calm back down.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: My performance reviews at work have slipped because I miss deadlines and avoid collaborative meetings, and socially I’ve pulled back so much from my Guatemalan community and even family visits that my husband and relatives keep asking why I’m never around anymore.\n- duration: These problems have been going on pretty steadily since I came back to New York     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Thank you, doctor. Honestly, I'm here because I find myself going out of my way to avoid absolutely anything that reminds me of what happened to my cousin. It's gotten to the point where I'm cutting out important parts of my own life just to keep from remembering.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you're reminded of what happened to your cousin, do you experience any distressing thoughts, feelings, or physical sensations?\n\nPatient: When I'm reminded, I suddenly see my cousin's body in the coffin or hear my aunt describing the shooting like a horrible clip playing in my head, and my chest tightens so much that I just freeze. I'm immediately flooded with this heavy dread and guilt, feeling like I somehow failed him by not getting him out of Guatemala sooner.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and images of your cousin's death, and have they been present every day since it happened?\n\nPatient: These intrusive images have been happening steadily since I came back from Guatemala after his funeral, which was a little over a year ago—about 14 months now. They happen almost every day, popping up out of nowhere while I'm at my desk working or just cooking dinner.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to perform specific rituals or repetitive behaviors in an attempt to reduce your distress or prevent something bad from happening, or are your actions mainly focused on avoiding reminders of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any rituals or repetitive behaviors like that. My actions are entirely focused on avoiding reminders of his death, so I stay off buses, avoid Spanish news, and have muted family group chats and Guatemalan community events. I do this because I am absolutely terrified that if I hear one more story or see one more image, I’ll completely fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge, having difficulty sleeping, or easily startled since your cousin's death, and if so, how have these symptoms affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm constantly on edge and lie in bed exhausted but wired, waking up from vivid dreams about buses and feeling completely unrefreshed. I also jump out of my skin at sudden noises or if someone walks up behind me at work, and it takes a long time to calm down. This constant exhaustion has caused my work performance to slip with missed deadlines, and I've pulled back from my family because I just have no energy left.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned avoiding reminders and having intrusive thoughts, but can you tell me if these thoughts are always about your cousin's death or if they sometimes involve fears of harm coming to yourself or others in a way that doesn't directly relate to your cousin's passing?\n\nPatient: Yes, the intrusive thoughts and mental images are always directly related to my cousin's death, like suddenly seeing him in his coffin or hearing my aunt describe the shooting. I don't have unrelated fears of harm coming to myself or other people, though I do carry a constant, heavy sense of dread and guilt specifically tied to feeling like I failed him.\n\nDoctor: Can you elaborate on what specifically makes you feel like you failed your cousin, is it something you did or didn't do before or after his death that contributes to this overwhelming sense of guilt?\n\nPatient: I feel a deep sense of guilt because I feel like I failed him by not being there when it happened, or by not helping him get out of Guatemala sooner. This heavy feeling of dread sits underneath everything I do now, coloring even my ordinary days so they all feel heavy and slightly wrong. It's just this constant weight that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that if you had acted differently, your cousin's death could have been prevented, and is this belief what drives your feeling of having failed him?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really believe that. I feel like if I had just helped him get out of Guatemala sooner, he would still be alive today. That deep conviction that I should have done something to prevent it is exactly what drives this constant guilt and makes every day feel so heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your guilt and belief about failing your cousin are realistic, or do you think they might be an exaggerated or distorted way of thinking about what happened?"

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
